# Economic Indicators  \#JobsDay analysis

Every month, the Bureau of Labor Statistics releases a report on the employment situation for the previous month. Their release includes data on job growth, unemployment, and wage growth, which gives us a snapshot of the health of the economy and whether it’s working for ordinary Americans.

### Key numbers _• October_

4.1%National unemployment rate

165,000Net jobs in October

80.7%Share of prime working-age population with a job

-333,000Net federal jobs since January 2025

Below, Economic Policy Institute senior economist Elise Gould offers her insights on the October jobs report released this morning. [Read the full thread here](https://shared.outlook.inky.com/link?domain=bsky.app&t=h.eJxtjksSgjAQRK9iZa2MSYAIK68SklFHAon5LMTy7oIbN666ul5Vv36xEh3rd-yWc0g9wJDGZ6VDgBD9hRwCOkp49cXZ6suSN6QdBJ8yyGmZJzHJR0nizvY7Nm5TaPzsJzKH4B2Z54HmlCmXjDCPuixORzK3MwaqfLxCN2itOKLlAo9C884atealts3JNLwBrjrZqVY1dXU6tq1s5WbCzfRnb2P2--LXae38_QESWUua.MEYCIQCiIo7BP06hJChZirQLZeaADd3gzHsrQd3RQwPuW6PA-wIhAItirNdBhD3ysAeAU_PwV4xKlDy5zsKlrV1Do2nyUvC3).

_After some weaker growth, today’s_ _[jobs report](https://shared.outlook.inky.com/link?domain=bsky.app&t=h.eJxtjkEOgyAURK9iWFcRFRFXvcoXfoVqhQAubNO7V7rppqvJy0xm5kX2sJKxICYlH0dKp7gcFXhPDUSTYKZ3N8WA3oVELgVZchaV29zDqtK71aqjtFtMNu0J6bbA_lwhWGWu6G3lwkzlBCAYomYN1g0wqZU49dZpPijOOGVCtlL0gnfVUPd927d5CfPSn77s6e-LH9uT2fsDS0RANg.MEYCIQDVGlWy_tYQk8cxT-5MP9Re-xZwKxfvndKX--CEt2CXCgIhAP212m1QYSedCYP9_1KupNWanY--onVM1SCi3rOUaNgy)_ _came in pretty strong. Healthy revisions to August and September meant 84,000 more jobs than originally reported. And today’s job growth of 165,000 puts an exclamation mark on those revisions. Employment is up an average of 137,000 jobs over the last three months._

_The household survey is also reporting labor market strength. The unemployment rate ticked down for the “right” reasons as the labor force and employment grew and the number of unemployed fell. The prime-age employment-to-population ratio remains high at 80.7%, just above its pre-pandemic level._

_Professional and business services surged after a much weaker year, rising 70,000 in October. Employment continued to grow in health care while leisure and hospitality recorded losses, erasing gains made in September._

_Federal employment dropped by 1,000 in October, and the deficit in the federal workforce remains stark: there are 333,000 fewer federal jobs today than in January 2025. The vital services federal employees provide cannot be done without these essential workers._

_With the recent gains in_ _[manufacturing](https://shared.outlook.inky.com/link?domain=bsky.app&t=h.eJxtjksOgyAYhK_SsK4iKiCuepVfQCEqEh4L2_TulW666WryZSYz80I5bGi8IZOSjyPGU1zPGrzHBqJJsOAdXJ5BphysW9D9htYS1_Jwx25l5Y_NyrOyLiabctLYrZCfGwQrzUN7Wx9hwWIC4ERrRVrdtECEkvzSuVd0kJRQTLjoBGec9vXQMNaxrizpsvSnr3jq--LH9mLy_gA3hEFg.MEYCIQDrGoVn9YE6O-ICg6GaeFsQcE8c3XnWvUOcUKiBK2R7hwIhAKXtCUeJUavj_MlFBdaoFIJq7H1BLr4iYrRsb5OmTDWy)_ _, jobs in the manufacturing sector have finally surpassed their level in January 2025. After tremendous losses through 2025, manufacturing employment is now just 8,000 above its January 2025 level. While welcome news, it’s hardly a manufacturing boon._

_Year-over-year nominal wage growth remains pretty weak, even though recent measures show some promise. Workers don’t have the leverage to bid up their wages. Even with low unemployment, the depressed hires rate means workers aren’t finding new jobs to raise their wages._

_Workers are losing the race between wages and prices. Nominal wage growth continues to decelerate as workers lose bargaining power at work. October price data out next week, but reduced leverage and higher prices has translated into falling year-over-year real wages for six months in a row._

## Charting jobs and unemployment

### Current Employment Statistics Charts

The Current Employment Statistics (CES) program, also known as the establishment survey, is a monthly survey administered by the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) which provides estimates of employment, hours, and earnings based on payroll records of approximately 131,000 businesses and government agencies.

### Current Population Survey Charts

The Current Population Survey (CPS), also known as the household survey, is sponsored jointly by the Census Bureau and the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS). Each month, it provides data on the labor force, employment, unemployment, persons not in the labor force, hours of work, earnings, and other demographic and labor force characteristics.

![Economic Policy Institute](https://www.epi.org/wp-content/themes/epi/img/logos/EPI40-wordmark-horizontal-no-tagline.png)

**Notes:** Data are seasonally adjusted. Seasonally adjusted data for Less than 5 weeks, 5-14 weeks, 15-26 weeks, and 27+ weeks are provided by BLS. Seasonally adjusted data are not available for 52+ so we applied the ratio of not-seasonally adjusted 52+ to not-seasonally adjusted 27+ to the seasonally adjusted 27+ to impute a seasonally adjusted 52+. Seasonally adjusted data for 27-51 is estimated as the difference between our estimated 52+ seasonally adjust data and the 27+ seasonally adjusted data.

**Source:** EPI analysis of Bureau of Labor Statistics Current Population Survey public data series.
